# Plan 55 - One page queries every ledger

**Last Updated**: 2026-10-07

**Level**: 5 (CLAUDE.md section 6). It adds a member to a persisted vocabulary (`RouteId` on the console band). It widens one persisted contract: `RawDayIndex` gains an optional `bytes` list, one size per listed file, so a browser can price a writer file and check its length before it fetches it (row 2). It adds an entry point to the query door, it publishes more of `state/` and the registry that names it, and it opens a surface where an operator's own text reaches a query engine. The site build writes listings in `RawDayIndex`'s shape into the staged site only, never into `state/` (row 7).

**Chain** (CLAUDE.md section 0d). **Intent**: the owner looks through the project's own recorded data directly - pick the ledgers, pick the days, write the question, read the answer - without waiting for somebody to build a panel for it. **Contract**: section 2 declares every address, shape, name, statement, knob, word and refusal these rows need, checked against `main` at 9cabfa8dd on 2026-10-02, so a worker writes each row with no further decision. **Code**: the seven rows.

Execute per docs/how-to/execute-a-plan.md: one owner carries the plan and delegates a row where delegation pays; keep parallel N = 2 rows in flight as a running pool, refilling a slot the moment a worker returns and never waiting on a merge; serialise merges and re-check each branch against the advanced main; run the browser and build gates one at a time behind the shared gate lock; consult a persona only where two answers would lead to different code; AUTO-merge on green gates where no ESCALATE trigger fired; honor the ESCALATE triggers in section 0. AUTHOR-AND-STOP until the user authorizes.

## 0. Operating contract

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Why this plan exists | Every figure on the console is a panel somebody wrote for one question. A question nobody anticipated has no surface, and answering it means a clone, a Python session and a morning. This plan gives the owner one page that reads every ledger the project declares, over any span including today, with the query written by hand and the answer drawn as a table and as a chart. |
| The page, in one sentence | **An empty shell that prerenders nothing.** It arrives with no data in it; the reader picks ledgers and days, types a query and presses run, or arrives on an address that carries a query and presses run themselves. |
| Hard scope - in | - `/console/data-explorer/` is a sixth console tab, labelled `Data explorer`, that **prerenders nothing** - a shell answered by the existing `404.html` fallback, which fetches everything it draws.<br>- The query door gains `ask()`: one read-only statement over a chosen set of ledgers and days, on the engine plan 51 shipped, unchanged.<br>- Every ledger `config/ledgers.json` declares reaches the page's picker; every published one is queryable; an unpublished one says so by name.<br>- A span reaches **today**: packed year, month and day files, and the writers' own files for days not packed yet, each listed by the site build with its size (row 7), with each date read through exactly one tier.<br>- **What the build copies to the site is capped at the widest span the console can ask for**, and a date older than the cap is read from the committed repository instead, reached through `From (UTC)` and `To (UTC)` dates on the page (row 6).<br>- The answer is a table and, where its columns can carry one, a chart from the vocabulary the chart-vocabulary row established.<br>- A query is saved in this browser, found again, and carried in the page address.<br>- The shared span control gets smaller and the console's default span becomes 14 days. |
| Hard scope - out | see the table below |
| ESCALATE triggers | 1. **The page prerenders, or a loader returns data to it.** Data is anything read from `state/` or from a published payload. The page's configuration - its knobs and the published list - arrives as build-time constants, as the root layout's `__UI_CONFIG__` does, and its `+page.ts` hands those constants to the console layout; that is configuration, not data. It ships an empty shell or it is not this page.<br>2. **`connect-src` gains any origin other than `ledger.archive_base_url`'s** (section 2.6 lists what it admits today), or the engine is handed a file the door did not choose - `registerFileURL` called anywhere but `registerAddress()` in `engine.ts`, an address handed to `registerAddress()` that `fetched-bytes.ts` did not compose, or a registered name taken from an index, a cell or a caller.<br>3. A statement the operator typed reaches the engine without passing `statement.ts`; the door sends a statement of its own other than the view statements in section 2.4 and the wraps in section 2.5 rules 7 and 8; or the door builds an identifier from anything but `LEDGER_NAMES`.<br>4. An address runs a query instead of loading it into the editor.<br>5. A ledger is published that `config/ledgers.json` does not declare. `LedgerConfig.published` is typed `list[LedgerName]`, so this fires only if a change loosens that type.<br>6. A second module imports `@duckdb/duckdb-wasm`, or the page composes an address under a state root - the site's, the raw tier's or the archive's - anywhere but the door's two address modules: the path in `slice-reader.ts` and the prefix, version and read part in `fetched-bytes.ts`, with each prefix bound in `ledger.ts`. The registry's address is composed in `registry.ts`, as `console/band.json` is composed in the console layout.<br>7. `ConsoleBand` changes beyond gaining one route id, its changelog entry, and the pointer entry that a fifth entry requires (CLAUDE.md section 11).<br>8. `RawDayIndex` changes beyond gaining the optional `bytes` list, its changelog entry, the three contract fixtures that carry the field, and its hand copy (row 2).<br><br>**A new chart type is not an escalation** - it is Susan's call on the vocabulary page. |
| Open questions | None. The owner ruled B1 on 2026-10-06: the column list always shows the selected ledgers' columns, and a run does not change it. |
| Decided 2026-10-02 | Four choices were open on the morning of 2026-10-02. Each is now a row decision, adopted to make the plan executable, and the owner may overrule any of them: where the browser learns which writer files a day not packed yet holds (row 7 decision 1); the optional `bytes` list on `RawDayIndex` (row 2 decision 11); the keep window `counterfactual-scores` publishes with (row 3 decision 12); and the custom UTC date range that makes the reach past the cap usable (row 6 decision 9, changed by the owner on 2026-10-03). |
| Chosen strategy | The chrome first and alone, because it shares a file with each of the next two; then the reader and the data together; then the listing of the days not packed yet, which needs the reader's contract and the data's copy step; then the page that needs all four; then what the page does with an answer; then the reach past the cap and the polish. Ruled by Fowler (CLAUDE.md section 14). |
| Execution | autonomous orchestrator per docs/how-to/execute-a-plan.md. Parallel N = 2, because rows 2 and 3 are the only file-disjoint pair and every other wave holds one row (section 1). |

### Hard scope - out

| What is out | What it costs to leave out | What would bring it in |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Taking the seven existing prerendered routes off prerender | `/`, `/archive/` and the five console pages keep their documents. Telemetry-intent N4 stays in tension with plan 26's ruling, and this plan does not resolve it - it only stops N4 being read as a bar on a page that bakes nothing | [`20260911-26-retire-prerender-plan.md`](20260911-26-retire-prerender-plan.md) section 6, which is already written as executable rows and needs only the owner's word. It was written when six routes prerendered; Judgement and Voices joined on 2026-09-12, so its rows cover five of the seven |
| A control on the page that widens `connect-src` | Nothing an operator can reach another way. The one capability the content policy genuinely removes is joining a ledger against a file of your own, and a file the **page** reads and hands the engine as a buffer restores it without widening anything. Reading past the site's cap needs no control either - row 6 does it with the page's own fetch | A config value in `config/idhazh.json`, which is where every origin on the list already comes from - never a control in the page, because a switch reached by a shared address is a switch a stranger's query can find already on |
| Dropping a local file onto the page and joining a ledger against it | You cannot compare a ledger against a spreadsheet you hold. It is the only real loss, and no boundary would need to move for it: the page reads the file with the browser's own file API and registers it as one more buffer | Its own row. About one component, one buffer registration and a column-type check, and it opens no network path |
| A download of any kind - CSV, Parquet, Arrow | You cannot open an answer in a spreadsheet. Row 5's copy-to-clipboard covers the case it was wanted for, and refusing a file also refuses the formula-injection surface a cell beginning `=` opens | Owner decision. It is one function and one button, and the cell-escaping rule comes with it |
| Writing anything back - a saved query committed to the repository, a query the pipeline runs | A saved query notices nothing when a column is renamed; it simply fails the next time you run it | A row that gives a saved query a file under `config/`, its own contract and a test that runs it |
| A second engine, a second dialect, or a query language of our own | One dialect. An operator learns DuckDB SQL, which they can use anywhere else | Nothing. This is a refusal, not a deferral |
| Charts outside the vocabulary page's list | The page draws what the console draws. An answer whose shape no type fits gets a table and a sentence saying why | Susan adds a type to the vocabulary page, which is a design call and not an escalation |
| Byte-range reads beyond the ones the door already makes | The door already reads a packed year file on the site by byte range (`RANGED_PERIODS` in `slice-reader.ts`, shipped by #1178), and `ask()` inherits that unchanged. Every file this plan adds - a writer file and an archive file - is fetched whole, so a wide archive span that reaches a year file fetches the whole year. Every fetch is bounded by the span control and refused past `console.explorer_max_fetch_bytes` | A measurement showing a real archive span the ceiling refuses that ranges would have answered. Note that the archive host's answer to a `HEAD` with a `Range` is not measured, and the engine opens a ranged file only from that answer |
| The lag between a day ending and its rows being packed | Nothing a reader can see: a span reaches the newest day the last deploy listed (row 7). The lag is a day or two for a ledger whose compaction runs live; it grows by a day every day for one whose compaction only reports (`dry_run` true in its `config/gardener/compact-<ledger>.json`); and the walk's cap of `max(console.window_presets)` days bounds what the site holds either way | - |

### The intent this plan serves

[docs/concepts/telemetry-intent.md](../docs/concepts/telemetry-intent.md) is the north star and sits above this plan (CLAUDE.md section 0d).

| # | The intent, in short | What this plan does about it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| N2 | The browser queries the parquet itself | **Delivered for arbitrary questions.** The door was built for panels; row 2 here opens it to a question nobody wrote a panel for |
| N3 | The browser fetches its own data at view time | **Delivered whole on one route.** This page fetches the ledger registry, the indexes and the data after mount, and bakes no data. Its knobs and the published list are configuration and arrive as build-time constants, the way `docs/concepts/config.md` says every knob a surface needs does |
| N4 | Prerendering is an anti-pattern | **This route never prerenders.** It is the first console route with no document. It does not remove the existing eight; plan 26 section 6 owns that |
| N5 | d3 is the only charting library | **Inherited.** Row 5 draws only from the vocabulary page's list |
| N7, N8 | `state/` is the only source; no production artefact under `frontend/` in git | **Advanced.** Row 3 publishes the ledgers themselves and the registry that names them; nothing new enters git |
| N9, N10, N11 | The name, the two tiers, the one shard pattern | **Inherited** from plan 50. This plan declares no new persisted shape. It widens two: `RouteId` gains one member (row 1), and `RawDayIndex` gains an optional `bytes` list, one size per name in `files`, so an older listing still validates (row 2). The site build writes listings in `RawDayIndex`'s shape into the staged site only, never into `state/` (row 7) |

**One pull request a row.** Row 1 runs first and alone. Rows 2 and 3 start together once it has merged. Row 7 waits for both, row 4 waits for row 7, and rows 5 and 6 follow one at a time. **Row 7 was added on 2026-10-02 and runs between rows 3 and 4**: its number is the order it was written in, not the order it runs in, and the group letters give the running order. **Row 11 was added on 2026-10-05, on the owner's ruling that day, and runs between rows 9 and 10**, because row 10 finishes the same two rails. **Row 12 was added the same day, on the owner's request that day, and runs between rows 11 and 10**, because row 10's density pass works inside the regions row 12 sizes. **Row 13 was added on 2026-10-05, from row 11's S7**: one type classification for the table, the chart and the colours, and a NULL that is no value in any chart. **Rows 19 and 20 were added on 2026-10-07 on the owner's A1+A2 ruling, specified by Susan and Jony.** **Rows 21 to 23 were added on 2026-10-07 at plan 62's request, designed by Fowler.** Rows 19 and 21 share no file and may run together. Row 20 is BLOCKED on the owner deciding whether this plan carries the small slice of plan 52 row 2 that its charts need. **Rows 22 and 23 were descoped on 2026-10-07 by the owner**, who kept the column list as it is: each ledger's columns are read from its newest file through the query engine. Row 21 stays, because it proves at each deploy that every published file carries the columns its contract declares.

**Readiness is the file-disjointness test, not the group letter** (execute-a-plan.md). The table below is the proof, recomputed on 2026-10-02 from the final `Files touched` lists of the third review cycle. Rows 2 and 3 are the only pair that runs at once, and no path appears under both. Every other wave holds one row.

**Row 1 runs first because it shares a file with each of the others.** With row 3 it shares `config/idhazh.json`: changing the default window to 14 days in `config/appearance.json` alone turns `test_a_moved_block_is_declared_in_one_file_and_the_other_does_not_argue` red, because `config/idhazh.json` still carries the legacy `console` block with `default_window_days` 30, and moving that value to 14 then turns `test_the_committed_config_caps_what_a_cold_console_load_fetches` red until `page_weight.cold_console_load_bytes` comes down. Row 3's first new `payload_ceilings_bytes` key, `config/ledgers.json`, sorts directly under that line, one unchanged line apart, which is too close to merge by luck. With row 2 it shares `docs/architecture/contracts/schemas.md`: row 1 lists the new `RouteId` binding and the 14-day default there, and row 2 lists the `RawDayIndex` hand copy and its new `bytes` field. **Holding rows 2 and 3 for row 1's merge is the price of those two shared files**, and row 1 is the smallest row in the plan.

**Row 2 touches no config file at all.** Its ceilings arrive as arguments (`AskOptions`, section 2.4) rather than as knobs it reads, so the knobs that bound the page are minted by the row that reads them - row 4 - and row 2's tests pass a literal. It learns how far each ledger's listings reach from the listings the site build staged (section 2.7), through a script of its own, `frontend/scripts/raw-listed-through.mjs`, so it edits no build script that row 3 edits.

**Row 7 runs alone, after rows 2 and 3.** It writes listings that carry the `bytes` field row 2 adds to the contract, its spec reads every listing through row 2's reader, and it extends the copy step row 3 edits. Run beside either of them, it would share a file or test against a contract that does not exist yet.

**The tab ships in row 1 behind a flag in `config/appearance.json`, and row 4 removes the flag.** Its removal condition is on its declaring line: row 4's merge.

**Rows 5 and 6 are single-slot waves on purpose.** Everything left after row 4 edits `frontend/src/routes/console/data-explorer/+page.svelte`, so a second worker there would be two workers on one file.

**Row 4 is the keystone and cannot be split.** It creates the route, the question panel, the answer panel, the ledger list, the editor and the table in one commit, because there is no intermediate state where the route exists and draws nothing - a console route that renders an empty page is a tab that lies. The chart panel joins in row 5, in the commit that draws it (section 2.2 row 5).

**What each wave costs while it runs.** The expensive gates - the build, the browser run - serialise behind the shared gate lock whatever the pool size says, so two slots buy two writers and not two builds.

## 2. The contracts

### 2.1 The door as it stands on `main`, which is what these rows extend

The query door shipped. **It is ten modules, not the three an earlier draft of this plan named**, and a row that edits the wrong one is a row that fails at its first import. This table is the map every frontend row reads first; it was re-read against `main` on 2026-10-02, after #1169 (the three ledgers published) and #1178 (a year file read by byte range) landed. No module has been added to `frontend/src/lib/data/` since 2026-09-29.

| # | Module | What it owns | What this plan does to it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `frontend/src/lib/data/ledger.ts` | The public face: `slice()`, `ledgerReach()`, the one page keeper, and the two bindings - the site prefix (`__ASSET_BASE_URL__ \|\| base`, handed to `fetchedBytes()`) and the engine (`browserEngine(__ENGINE_EXTENSION_REPOSITORY__)`) | **Row 2 adds `ask()` and `askCost()` here, and binds `__RAW_LISTED_THROUGH__` (section 2.7).** Row 4 adds `startAfresh()` (section 2.9). Row 6 binds a second byte source over the archive prefix here |
| 2 | `frontend/src/lib/data/slice-shapes.ts` | `LedgerName`, `LEDGER_NAMES`, `LEDGER_FAULTS`, `LedgerFault`, `DateStamp`, `Row`, `Predicate`, `SliceOptions`, `SliceResult`, `SliceRequestError`, `COLUMN_NAME`, `isDay()`, `checkedRequest()` | **Row 2 widens `LEDGER_NAMES` from its three published names to every ledger `config/ledgers.json` declares** and adds the `ask()` shapes |
| 3 | `frontend/src/lib/data/slice.ts` | Pure: `daysBetween()`, `firstNamed()`, `filesFor()` - for each day, the coarsest packed file that holds it (year, then month, then day), or the first hole | **`filesFor()` is not changed**, because `slice()` calls it and panels stay on packed files. Row 2 adds a second pure function beside it for the writers' own files; row 6 adds the archive's selection |
| 4 | `frontend/src/lib/data/slice-query.ts` | `QueryEngine` (with the optional `registerAddress()`), `OpenedAddress`, `statementFor()`, `rowsFor()`, `cellOf()`, `SliceValueError`, `DATE_COLUMN`, and the module-private `listOf()` | Row 2 exports `listOf()` unchanged and adds nothing to `statementFor()`. **`ask()` does not use `cellOf()`**: in the shipped engine a sum or a decimal reaches JavaScript as an object, a list or a struct as an object, and a date as milliseconds, and `cellOf()` throws on every object (section 2.5 rule 8) |
| 5 | `frontend/src/lib/data/page-keeper.ts` | `pageKeeper()`, `ByteSource` (with the optional `address()`), `WantedFile` (with `byRange`), `Holding`, `FileShortfall`. `hold()` fetches and registers a whole file once for the page's life, and opens a `byRange` file at an address for one call alone; the caller calls the `done()` it returns once its statements end | **Row 2 calls `hold()` and `done()` and never registers a file itself.** Row 4 changes the module note that says a page never releases its keeper, because Refresh does (section 2.9) |
| 6 | `frontend/src/lib/data/engine.ts` | The only importer of `@duckdb/duckdb-wasm`. `browserEngine()`, `nodeEngine()`, minted names `door/<n>.parquet`; `register()` takes a buffer, and `registerAddress()` opens a year file at a URL by byte range | **Untouched.** No setting, no pre-load, no pin (section 2.6) |
| 7 | `frontend/src/lib/data/compact-index.ts` | The hand copy of `CompactIndex` / `CompactEntry` and the stamp this build reads | Untouched. Row 2 writes the hand copy of `RawDayIndex`, its stamp and the file-name grammar in a module of its own, `frontend/src/lib/data/raw-day-index.ts`, because this module answers one question - can this build read a compact index - and a second contract would widen it (CLAUDE.md section 1a). `test_frontend_index_shapes.py` reads both files |
| 8 | `frontend/src/lib/data/fetched-bytes.ts` | `fetchedBytes(prefix, fetcher)`: the `ByteSource` over HTTP, rooted at `<prefix>/state/`, which composes every data address (`?v=<rows>-<bytes>`, and `&read=<random>` for a ranged read) | Untouched by row 2. Row 6 calls it a second time, over the archive prefix |
| 9 | `frontend/src/lib/data/slice-reader.ts` | `readSlice()`, `indexPath()`, `dataPath()`, `RANGED_PERIODS`, `faultLine()` - the one place a path under the state root is composed | **Row 2 adds the two raw-tier paths here** (the day's listing and a writer file); row 6 reuses the compact paths for the archive |
| 10 | `frontend/src/lib/data/ledger-reach.ts`, `frontend/src/lib/server/ledger-disk.ts` | How far a ledger reaches; `sliceFromDisk()`, the build-time reader | Untouched |

**Four facts about the shipped engine decide most of section 2.6.**

| # | Fact, read from `engine.ts` on `main` | What follows |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **The parquet reader is an add-on the engine downloads at first use**, from `ledger.engine_extension_repository`, which is `https://extensions.duckdb.org` in `config/idhazh.json` | The engine needs the network to work at all, so a database setting that closes it is a setting that breaks every panel. This is the first of the two reasons section 2.6 adds no engine setting |
| 2 | **The browser worker starts from a `blob:` bootstrap, so it inherits the page's `connect-src`** | The browser's own policy is already the boundary on the engine, not only on the page - the second reason. Section 2.6 lists the origins it admits today |
| 3 | **`register()` mints the name** `door/<n>.parquet` from a counter, and a registered buffer is a **file**, not a table | A statement cannot say `FROM "item-health"` until something creates that name. Section 2.4 is where it is created |
| 4 | **`registerAddress()` calls `registerFileURL()`** with the HTTP protocol, for a year file the door reads by byte range, and the database opens with `forceFullHTTPReads` off (#1178) | The engine's worker itself fetches a year file from this site. The URL is composed by `fetched-bytes.ts` from a closed ledger name and an index-checked period, and the name is minted, so this is a shipped, bounded exception to "the engine fetches nothing": ESCALATE trigger 2 names it rather than forbidding it |

### 2.2 The address, the tab, and the route id

| # | What | Value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Address | `/console/data-explorer/` |
| 2 | Route id | `data-explorer`, a member of `RouteId` in `backend/idhazh/contracts/console_band.py` and of the `RouteId` union in `frontend/src/lib/console/band.ts` |
| 3 | Tab label | `Data explorer` |
| 4 | Tab description | `What the ledgers hold, and whatever you ask of them.` The other five descriptions each say what their route holds, and an instruction would break that family as a verb in the label would (row 1 decision 6). Susan, 2026-10-02 |
| 5 | Panel group | one group, id `data-explorer`, title `""` - the Pipelines shape, because what the three panels need is an order rather than a grouping. **Row 4 registers it with two panels, `data-explorer-ask` and `data-explorer-rows`. Row 5 adds `data-explorer-shape` to it, to both contract fixtures and to the captures, in the commit that draws that panel.** `frontend/tests/panel-captures.spec.ts` pictures every panel `console.panel_groups` names and `frontend/tests/support/console-panels.ts` refuses a route key with no address, so a group registered before its route exists is a red check - and a chart panel registered in row 4 would be a box on a live page that promises a chart and draws none until row 5 (Jony, 2026-10-02) |
| 6 | Panel ids and titles, in order | `data-explorer-ask`, titled `Your question`; `data-explorer-rows`, titled `The answer`; `data-explorer-shape`, titled `The answer, drawn` (Susan, 2026-10-02). `Panel` requires a title. The question panel's note is section 2.16 rule 1; the answer panel's and the chart panel's notes are section 2.11 |
| 7 | Arrangement | **Three panels in one column at every width, each as wide as the frame: the question, then the answer, then the chart.** No two panels ever stand side by side. The answer comes before the chart because every run has an answer and many have no chart, so a "nothing to draw" sentence never stands between the reader and the rows. The answer table scrolls inside a box of limited height (section 2.11, answer-panel rule 1), so the chart starts within one box below it (Jony, 2026-10-02) |
| 8 | The cross-link sentence | Removed on the Data explorer by the workbench chrome. The Pipelines tab is the link, and the route keeps no `[data-console-carry]` line. This replaces the 2026-10-02 ruling (Jony and Susan's redesign, 2026-10-04, on the owner's direction that this page drops the console band) |
| 9 | The band's worst state for this route | Always none: the route judges nothing, so `console_band.py` puts `RouteId.DATA_EXPLORER: None` beside the other routes' worst states |

`RouteId` holds five members today - `pipelines`, `model`, `machine`, `judgement`, `voices`. **The id and the label differ on purpose, and that is the established pattern**: `model` is labelled `Summaries` and `machine` is labelled `Hardware`.

**Adding a route id is additive and has a precedent in the same file.** `ConsoleBand.__changelog__` already carries `2026-09-12 - RouteId gains the route ids judgement and voices. Additive: an older payload names three of the five and validates unchanged.` Row 1 adds one entry of the same shape at the top; `version` is the newest entry's date, so it moves with no other edit. **The changelog holds four entries today, so the new one is the fifth change:** CLAUDE.md section 11 allows four changes and one pointer, so the oldest entry (`2026-09-09`, the initial shape) becomes `Earlier changes are in this file's git history.`, spelled as the other contracts spell it. A band payload written before this row names five routes and still validates.

**What an old payload draws.** `readBand()` in `band.ts` draws one tab for every id in its own `ROUTE_IDS`, in that order, and fills a route the payload omits from `ROUTE_WORDS` with no worst state. So after row 1 the strip has six tabs whatever the payload names, and `console.data_explorer_tab` is what hides the sixth until row 4.

**Nothing binds the frontend's copy of `RouteId` today.** `band.ts` types `RouteId` and `ROUTE_IDS` by hand and no backend test reads them. Row 1 adds that binding to `backend/tests/contracts/test_frontend_console_lists.py`: `ROUTE_IDS` names `RouteId`'s members in the order `ROUTES` in `telemetry/publish/console_band.py` writes them (CLAUDE.md section 1a).

### 2.3 This route prerenders nothing, and that is a shipped pattern rather than a new argument

`frontend/src/routes/console/data-explorer/+page.ts` declares both, and a universal `load` that returns configuration and nothing else:

```ts
export const prerender = false;
export const ssr = false;

/** The console layout draws the span control and the completeness sentence from
 *  `page.data.console`, which every other console route's server load supplies.
 *  This route has no server load, so it hands the layout the same knobs from the
 *  build-time constant. Its own knobs and the published list ride beside them,
 *  and the layout reads neither. */
export function load() {
	return { console: __CONSOLE_CONFIG__, explorer: __EXPLORER_CONFIG__, publishedLedgers: __PUBLISHED_LEDGERS__ };
}
```

| # | Fact | Read from |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The static adapter already has a fallback | `frontend/svelte.config.js`: `adapter({ fallback: '404.html', strict: false })` |
| 2 | Two routes already ship no document and are answered by that fallback | `frontend/src/routes/[date]/+page.ts` and `[date]/[vertical]/+page.ts`, both `export const ssr = false`, each with a universal `load` that returns no data |
| 3 | What the fallback costs | GitHub Pages serves `404.html` with an HTTP 404 status. The dated routes have lived with it since 2026-09-09 |
| 4 | What this route does **not** cost | Every item in plan 26 section 6 is about converting one of the existing prerendered routes - seven today: `/`, `/archive/` and the five console pages: re-homing a server load, re-pricing a ceiling that measures prerendered HTML, rewriting a spec that reads the prerendered document. A route that never prerendered has none of those, and this route has no `+page.server.ts` at all |
| 5 | The fallback carries the content policy | SvelteKit emits the `kit.csp` meta tag into `404.html` as into a prerendered page: a local build of 2026-10-02 (06:29Z) carries `connect-src 'self' https://huggingface.co https://us.aws.cdn.hf.co https://extensions.duckdb.org` there. So `connect-src` binds this page and its engine worker as it binds a prerendered one. Row 2's browser check is what proves it for the engine |
| 6 | Where the three constants come from | Row 4 adds them to `frontend/vite.config.ts` and `frontend/src/app.d.ts`, beside `__UI_CONFIG__` and `__ENGINE_EXTENSION_REPOSITORY__`: `__CONSOLE_CONFIG__`, what `consoleConfig()` in `frontend/src/lib/server/config.ts` resolves; `__EXPLORER_CONFIG__`, from a new `explorerConfig()` in the same file, which reads the `explorer_*` keys from the `console` block as `panelGroupsFor()` reads `panel_groups`; and `__PUBLISHED_LEDGERS__`, from `publishedLedgers()`, which `frontend/scripts/published-ledgers.mjs` already exports, so `ledger.published` keeps one reader. **The explorer knobs stay out of `ConsoleConfig`**: what `consoleConfig()` returns is inlined into all five prerendered console documents and keeps the declared interface and nothing else, so five example statements would otherwise ride in every console page. A universal module may not import `$lib/server/`, which is why the root layout's knobs travel the same way |
| 7 | What a reader with JavaScript off sees | This page is the fallback document, so it shows only the `<noscript>` line in `frontend/src/app.html`, which today says "A page for one day opens with JavaScript" - false here. Row 4 rewrites it so it is true on every page the fallback serves: `This page opens with JavaScript. Without it, the front page still opens on the head of the newest digest, and every published day is listed in the archive.`, keeping its two links (Susan, 2026-10-02; Jony asked for the same fix with a third link, to the console, and the words are Susan's call) |

**The page's only data is what it fetches.** No loader returns data, so there is nothing a build could bake in, which is the property telemetry-intent N4 is actually about. Its configuration is baked, as every page's is (`docs/concepts/config.md`, "Build-time config versus shipped config"). The route hands its span to the layout's control through `fillWindowSlot()` in `frontend/src/lib/console/window-slot.ts`, as every console route does. No other plan's trigger fires and this plan edits none of them: the count of prerendered routes does not move.

### 2.4 `ask()`: the signature, and how a buffer becomes a table

Row 2 adds two exports to `frontend/src/lib/data/ledger.ts`, `ask()` and `askCost()`, and their shapes to `slice-shapes.ts`. Everything already there is unchanged.

```ts
/** Run one read-only statement over a chosen set of ledgers and days.
 *  The caller chooses the ledgers and the span; the statement shapes the answer
 *  and never chooses a file. */
export function ask(opts: AskOptions): Promise<AskResult>;

export type AskOptions = {
	/** Non-empty. Each becomes one view of the same name, hyphens and all -
	 *  a statement quotes it as "item-health". */
	ledgers: readonly LedgerName[];
	from: DateStamp;
	to: DateStamp;
	/** One read-only statement. Refused before the engine sees it if it is not. */
	sql: string;
	/** The longest statement `statement.ts` lets through, in characters. */
	maxChars: number;
	/** The most rows the answer holds. Past it `capped` is true and the rows are real. */
	maxRows: number;
	/** The most bytes this call may fetch. Refused before any fetch when the
	 *  indexes say the span costs more. */
	maxFetchBytes: number;
};

/** One column of the answer, as the engine describes it. */
export type Column = { name: string; type: string };

/** What the fetch actually cost, so the page prints a reading and never a guess. */
export type FetchCost = { files: number; bytes: number; alreadyHeld: number; ms: number };

/** What a span will cost before it is paid, from the indexes and the day listings alone,
 *  and how far each selected, published ledger reaches: its newest day that holds a file, packed or listed. */
export type SpanCost = { files: number; bytes: number; unpackedDays: readonly DateStamp[]; through: Readonly<Partial<Record<LedgerName, DateStamp>>> };

export type AskResult =
	| { state: 'ok'; columns: readonly Column[]; rows: Row[]; capped: boolean; read: FetchCost; unpackedDays: readonly DateStamp[] }
	| { state: 'quiet'; columns: readonly Column[]; read: FetchCost }
	| { state: 'missing'; ledger: LedgerName }
	| { state: 'unreachable'; ledger: LedgerName | null; at: DateStamp | null; fault: AskFault }
	| { state: 'refused'; because: AskRefusal };

/** Why an answer did not come. `null`: a file the indexes name did not arrive whole.
 *  A `LedgerFault` other than `not-packed`: a gap in what the site holds. Both name `ledger` and `at`.
 *  `engine`: the query engine did not start in this browser, and `ledger` and `at` are null. */
export type AskFault = Exclude<LedgerFault, 'not-packed'> | 'engine' | null;

/** Why a statement did not run, with the figures its sentence prints (section 2.12, the words table). */
export type AskRefusal =
	| { kind: 'statements'; count: number }
	| { kind: 'not-read-only'; word: string }
	| { kind: 'too-long'; chars: number; max: number }
	| { kind: 'over-ceiling'; bytes: number; files: number; max: number }
	| { kind: 'engine-error'; message: string };

/** What the span would cost, from the indexes and the day listings, fetching no data file. */
export function askCost(ledgers: readonly LedgerName[], from: DateStamp, to: DateStamp): Promise<SpanCost>;
```

**`unreachable` and `refused` carry what their sentences print, and the door writes no sentence.** The page draws section 2.12's words from these fields, so `waiting.ts` stays the one place that holds a state's words. A browser with no WebAssembly exception handling is an engine that did not start (`fault: 'engine'`): telling the two apart would need a change to `engine.ts`, which this plan keeps untouched, and the one sentence for both says what the reader can do.

**`ask` rather than `query`.** The module is already the query door, so `query()` beside `slice()` would put the same word in two jobs one sentence apart (CLAUDE.md section 0b).

**A registered buffer is a file, so `ask()` creates one view per ledger.** The engine mints every registered name - `door/<n>.parquet` from a counter - and a statement cannot name a ledger until something binds the name.

**Why the registered name is not simply the ledger's name.** Not for a security reason first, but an arithmetic one: **one ledger is many files.** Fourteen days of `item-health` is one month file, plus the day files after it, plus the writers' files for the days not packed yet, and five parquet blobs cannot share one name. The minted name names a **file**; the ledger name names the **set**. The view is the one statement that binds the second to the first, and the minting rule then costs nothing, because the name a person types was never going to be the name a file was registered under.

```ts
// For each selected ledger, in order, after `hold()` has returned its names:
//   CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW "item-health" AS
//     SELECT * FROM read_parquet(['door/1.parquet', 'door/2.parquet'], union_by_name = true)
// A month or year file the span reads only some days of gets a read of its own (rule 9):
//   CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW "item-health" AS
//     SELECT * FROM read_parquet(['door/1.parquet'], union_by_name = true)
//       WHERE "covers" >= '2026-08-31' AND "covers" <= '2026-08-31'
//     UNION ALL BY NAME
//     SELECT * FROM read_parquet(['door/2.parquet', 'door/3.parquet'], union_by_name = true)
// For every name in LEDGER_NAMES that this call did NOT select:
//   DROP VIEW IF EXISTS "item-health"
```

| # | Rule about the views | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The view name is a member of `LEDGER_NAMES` and nothing else, double-quoted | A closed union is the only identifier source. No typed text and no fetched text becomes an identifier (Guardrail #11) |
| 2 | The file list is the minted names `hold()` returned, each quoted as a string literal, through `listOf()`, which row 2 exports from `slice-query.ts` unchanged | The engine minted every one of them |
| 3 | **Every unselected ledger's view is dropped on every call** | A view left behind from an earlier run points at files the keeper still holds, so a statement could read a ledger the operator did not select and did not see the cost of. Dropping is one statement per unselected name and it runs before the operator's statement |
| 4 | `union_by_name = true`, matching the panel path | A day written before a column existed reads that column as null rather than failing the read |
| 5 | The door's own view statements are not subject to section 2.6 control 4 | That control reads the operator's text. The door's statements are built from a closed union and minted names |
| 6 | The views are made after `hold()` returns, the statement runs, and then `done()` is called, answered or not | `done()` drops a year file opened at an address for this call alone (section 2.1 module 5). A view over a dropped name is rebuilt or dropped by the next call before its statement runs |
| 7 | **`ask()` runs one call at a time.** A call starts only after the previous call's `done()` has run | Each call rewrites every view, and the engine has one connection. Two calls at once - and the page describes each selected ledger with a call of its own - could drop each other's view between creating it and reading it |
| 8 | **A selected ledger with no file in the span gets a view over its own `through` day's files, with `LIMIT 0`.** The describe before the run already holds those files. When no selected ledger holds a file in the span, `ask()` answers `quiet` and starts no engine, as `slice()` does | `read_parquet` refuses an empty file list (DuckDB 1.5.5: "read_parquet needs at least one file to read"), so without this rule the engine's error would reach the page as `refused`. It happens today: `candidate-models` has packed nothing since 2026-09-08 |
| 9 | **A month or year file is read for its own days of the span.** `filesFor()` picks the coarsest file that holds a day, so a span that starts or ends inside a month reads that month's file, and the archive and the site can both hold a month that crosses the site's oldest day. Such a file gets a read of its own, `WHERE "covers" >= '{first}' AND "covers" <= '{last}'`, and the reads join `UNION ALL BY NAME`. Neighbouring files read whole - a day file, a writer's file, a month or year file wholly inside its days - share one read, as rule 2 says. Each day is checked as `YYYY-MM-DD` before it is written into the statement, because it comes from an index | `covers` is the day a row was filed under: every packed row carries it, even inside a month or year file, and the files and the two keepers divide a ledger's days by it. `date` is not on every ledger - `published` and `seen` have none - so a filter on it fails every question over them. Without the filter, an answer holds rows from outside its span, and a day both keepers hold is counted twice. On the site a year file is opened by address, and the same filter lets the engine skip its row groups outside the span |

**`LEDGER_NAMES` widens to every ledger `config/ledgers.json` declares.** It stays a hand-written closed union in `slice-shapes.ts`. Two existing tests read it, and row 2 changes both:

| # | Test, on `main` | What it holds today | What row 2 makes it hold |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `test_every_ledger_the_door_may_query_is_a_ledger` in `backend/tests/contracts/test_frontend_index_shapes.py` | Every name in `LEDGER_NAMES` is a `LedgerName` | The two sets are equal. `test_the_committed_registry_names_every_ledger_exactly_once` in `test_ledger_registry.py` already holds `LedgerName` equal to the registry, so the union is bound to the registry in both directions with no new test file |
| 2 | `test_every_ledger_the_door_can_be_asked_for_is_published` in `backend/tests/contracts/test_published_ledgers_cover_the_panels.py` | Every name in `LEDGER_NAMES` is in `ledger.published` - "a ledger added to the set without being published is a call that compiles and then answers 404" | Deleted. This page names an unpublished ledger on purpose and answers `missing` without asking the site (section 2.9). The panel half of that file, `test_every_ledger_a_panel_asks_the_door_for_is_published`, stays and is what still stops a panel's `slice()` or `ledgerReach()` naming an unpublished ledger |

A panel still names a ledger and never a path.

**The columns come from the engine and from nowhere else.** The column list always reads the selected ledgers' own columns, which `ask()` takes from `DESCRIBE` over one day of each selected ledger. The answer's own columns stay visible in the answer table's typed header. The page calls `askCost()` for the selected ledgers, then `ask()` with that ledger alone, `from` and `to` both that ledger's day in `SpanCost.through`, and `DESCRIBE "<ledger>"` as the statement. That fetches the files of one day per ledger - the newest day that holds a file, which every span ending today reads again - and the action line says so while it happens (section 2.16 rule 5). The keeper keeps them for the run. **The page calls neither `slice()` nor `ledgerReach()`**: `test_published_ledgers_cover_the_panels.py` refuses a `slice()` or `ledgerReach()` call whose ledger is not a literal anywhere under `frontend/src/` outside `lib/data/`, and every ledger here is one a person picked. There is no second copy of a ledger's column list to keep in step. Owner ruling, 2026-10-06.

### 2.5 What bounds the bytes

| # | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | The caller names the ledgers and the days. **A span ends on the reader's UTC day, read with `Date.now()` and the `getUTC*` accessors** (CLAUDE.md section 2); row 6 lets it end on an earlier day (section 2.8). `filesFor()` in `slice.ts` turns the packed part of that into a file set exactly as it does for a panel, and the writers'-files function beside it (section 2.7) adds the days after the newest packed day, through the newest day the build listed |
| 2 | Each file goes through `pageKeeper.hold()`. A day, month or writer file is fetched whole and handed to the engine once for the page's life; a year file is read by byte range for this call alone, as a panel reads one. **`ask()` never calls `engine.register()` or `engine.registerAddress()` itself** - the keeper is what stops a file crossing the network or entering the engine twice |
| 3 | **The statement never names a file, a path or a URL.** It sees view names and nothing else |
| 4 | `askCost()` reads the indexes and, for days not packed yet, the day listings, and answers what the span will fetch. A packed file counts at its index entry's `bytes`, a writer file at its size in its listing's `bytes` list (section 2.7), and a year file read by range at its entry's `bytes`, which is the most the read can cost. The page prints it **before** the run. `ask()` refuses past `maxFetchBytes` with `refused` and an `over-ceiling` reason carrying the bytes, the files and the ceiling, **before any data file is fetched** |
| 5 | Guardrail #12 is satisfied by the span, not by the archive: before a run the page fetches one day of each chosen ledger to list its columns (section 2.4), and a run fetches the days of its span and no more, however long the project has been running |
| 6 | **A browser page keeps every whole file it has fetched for the page's life** (`page-keeper.ts`), so bytes accumulate across runs on one page. `maxFetchBytes` bounds one call; the reload bounds the page. Row 4 prints the running total in the action line (section 2.16 rule 5), so the ceiling is never the first news of it |
| 7 | **The row ceiling is applied in the engine, and the statement sits on lines of its own.** `ask()` removes a final `;` that only spaces or comments follow. It then runs `SELECT COLUMNS(*)::VARCHAR FROM (`, a line break, the statement, a line break, and `) LIMIT <maxRows + 1>`. On one line, a statement ending in a `--` comment would comment out the closing bracket: DuckDB 1.5.5 and DuckDB-Wasm 1.33.1-dev57 both answer "syntax error at end of input". `SELECT`, `WITH ... SELECT`, `DESCRIBE` and `SUMMARIZE` parse inside this wrap. `EXPLAIN` does not, so it runs as typed and is read whole. One more row than the ceiling sets `capped`, and the extra row is dropped. `QueryEngine.rows()` returns a whole result, so a cap applied afterwards would still carry every row out of the engine's worker |
| 8 | **Every cell crosses as text, and the column types come from `DESCRIBE`.** Before the statement runs, `ask()` runs `DESCRIBE SELECT * FROM (`, a line break, the statement, a line break, `)`. That binds the statement without running it. Its `column_name` and `column_type` become `AskResult.columns`, including for a quiet answer, which has no row to read names from. `EXPLAIN` always answers two text columns. `ask()` does not use `cellOf()`: in the shipped engine a `sum()` or a decimal reaches JavaScript as an object (`1.5` as a decimal whose JSON is `"15"`), a list or a struct as an object, and a date as milliseconds, and `cellOf()` throws on every object. `answer.ts` turns a text cell back into a number only where its column type is integer, float or decimal and the text is a finite, safe number. The wrap also renames a repeated column (`id`, `id_1`); unwrapped, `rows()` would keep only the last `id` |

### 2.6 The boundary is the browser's, and this plan adds nothing to it

**This is the design plan 51 approved and shipped, unchanged. No row here sets an engine option, pins the engine's version, or pre-loads an add-on** (owner, 2026-09-29).

A database setting would have been a second lock on a door the browser already holds shut, and it would have broken the door on the way: the engine links only DuckDB's core functions and fetches its parquet reader over the network at first use, so a setting that closes the network closes every panel on the console (section 2.1 facts 1 and 2). **A static site's boundary is its content policy. That is how every site does this, and this one is not special.**

**Four controls, and three of them are already on `main`.**

| # | Control | What it stops | Where it lives |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Only files the door chose are visible: a buffer the page fetched (`registerFileBuffer`), or a year file opened at an address `fetched-bytes.ts` composed from a closed ledger name and an index-checked period (`registerFileURL`, inside `registerAddress()` alone, #1178) | The engine cannot open a file the door did not choose | `engine.ts`, shipped |
| 2 | **Every registered name is minted by the engine** - `door/<n>.parquet` from a counter, never from an index, a `covers` value, a cell or a caller | No fetched text and no typed text can name a file | `engine.ts`, shipped |
| 3 | `connect-src`, which the browser enforces **on the engine's worker as well as on the page**, because the worker starts from a `blob:` bootstrap | Any request to an origin not on the list, from the page or from inside a statement | `asset-base.js` computes each origin and `svelte.config.js` hands the list to `connectSources()`, shipped |
| 4 | The page refuses, before the engine sees it: more than one statement; anything that is not `SELECT`, `WITH ... SELECT`, `DESCRIBE`, `SUMMARIZE` or `EXPLAIN`; and a statement longer than `AskOptions.maxChars`, which row 4 fills from `console.explorer_query_max_chars` | A write, a schema change, and a paste large enough to stop the tab responding | `statement.ts`, row 2 |

**Control 4 is a courtesy, and it is written down as one.** It gives the operator a fast, readable refusal for an obvious mistake, in their own words rather than the engine's. A denylist over a language with function syntax is not a boundary, and **nothing in this plan rests on it**.

**What `connect-src` admits, stated rather than implied.** `connectSources()` in `frontend/asset-base.js` computes it from config. On 2026-10-02 the committed config yields `'self'`, the encoder's two failover origins `https://huggingface.co` and `https://us.aws.cdn.hf.co` (`encoderOrigins()`), and `https://extensions.duckdb.org` (`engineOrigins()`); `visuals.asset_base_url`'s origin joins it when that knob is set, and it is empty today. Row 3 adds `ledger.archive_base_url`'s origin the same way - a reader in `asset-base.js`, joined to the list `svelte.config.js` hands `connectSources()` - and it ships empty. **A statement can read a public file on one of those origins and nowhere else.** That is the whole residual. It is the same exposure every panel on the console already has, it is what a browser can actually enforce, and no row here widens it past the one address the owner asked for.

**The engine takes a caret range, not a pin.** There is no setting whose removal could break a boundary, because no boundary here is a setting.

**What the oracle asserts, and what it deliberately does not.** Row 2 proves the boundary that exists. The first line is the browser check in row 2's gates, because no Node run has a content policy; the other three are the Node oracle:

```
SELECT * FROM read_csv('https://example.invalid/x.csv')   -- the browser refuses the request
SELECT 1; DROP VIEW "item-health"                         -- statement.ts: two statements
CREATE TABLE t AS SELECT 1                                -- statement.ts: not read-only
SELECT * FROM "summary-quality-evals"                     -- with only host-fingerprint selected: the engine names no such table
```

**It does not assert that `INSTALL httpfs` fails.** With no seal it may well succeed, because the extension host is one of the admitted origins - and succeeding buys nothing, since every request the loaded add-on could then make is still refused by the same policy. **An oracle that asserted a refusal we do not enforce would be a test that passes for the wrong reason** and goes red on an engine upgrade that changed nothing that matters.

**For the same reason it does not assert that a minted file name is unreadable.** `SELECT * FROM 'door/1.parquet'` reads that file: the keeper keeps every whole file it fetched registered under its minted name for the page's life, and DuckDB reads a registered file named in a `FROM`. What it reaches is rows this page already fetched and counted, so nothing rests on hiding it, and no control here claims to. The view-drop rule (section 2.4 rule 3) is about the cost a person sees, and a held file costs nothing more.

### 2.7 A span reaches today, through three tiers of file

**Each date is read through exactly one tier.** The door takes, per date, the first of these that covers it. The packed tier is `filesFor()` as it stands, unchanged; row 2 adds the writers' tier in a second pure function beside it in `slice.ts`, which only `ask()` calls; row 6 adds the archive.

| # | Tier | Address | When it covers a date | Row |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Packed: year, month or day | `state/compact/<ledger>/yearly/<YYYY>/<YYYY>.parquet`, `monthly/<YYYY>/<MM>.parquet` or `daily/<YYYY>/<MM>/<DD>.parquet` | An index on the site names the date's year, month or day; the coarsest wins, and a year file is read by byte range | shipped |
| 2 | The writers' own files | The day's listing at `state/raw/<ledger>/index/<YYYY-MM-DD>.json`, a `RawDayIndex` that carries each file's size; then every file it names, at `state/raw/<ledger>/<YYYY>/<MM>/<DD>/<file name>` | The date is after the newest day any packed index names - a day as itself, a month through its last UTC day and a year through 31 December - and on or before the newest day the build listed for that ledger | 2 reads it; 7 stages the listing |
| 3 | The repository | `<ledger.archive_base_url>/state/compact/<ledger>/...`, chosen from the repository's own three indexes at that address | The date is before the oldest day the site's indexes name (section 2.8), and the repository's indexes name it | 6 |

**Why the site build writes the listing.** `RawDayIndex` is declared in `backend/idhazh/contracts/ledger_index.py`: the ledger, the date, the file names in ascending order, their digest, and when they were listed. The gardener writes one to `state/raw/<ledger>/index/<YYYY-MM-DD>.json` - **but only when it packs that day**: `backend/idhazh/gardener/tasks/_index_day.py` says the compaction lists a day the moment it takes it and nothing else writes a listing. So a day not packed yet has no listing, and the browser cannot list a directory. The writer files themselves are parquet (`ledger.compression_raw` is snappy), so the engine reads them as it reads a packed file. **Row 7 has the site build write the listing** (row 7 decision 1), and row 2 gives the contract the one field the browser needs from it (row 2 decision 11).

| # | Rule about the listing | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **Which days.** Per published ledger, every day after the newest day any packed index names - a day as itself, a month through its last UTC day and a year through 31 December - through the newest of those days that has a directory under `state/raw/<ledger>/`. At most `max(console.window_presets)` days are listed, counted from the day after that newest packed day. A day in that range with no directory gets a listing that names no file, which the contract allows | **The walk is bounded by the widest preset, whatever packing does.** On 2026-10-02 `summary-quality-evals` packs in report-only mode (`dry_run` true in `config/gardener/compact-summary-quality-evals.json`) and gains an unpacked day every day until plan 57's first row switches it on, and `candidate-models` has packed nothing since 2026-09-08. **The anchor is the data, never the build's clock**: counted from the build's day, the canary build, whose data is fixed in August 2026, would stage one more empty listing per ledger every day |
| 2 | **What each listing carries.** The day's file names in ascending order; their digest as the contract defines it, SHA-256 of the names joined by one newline; each file's size in `bytes`, in the same order as the names; and `listed_at`, the build's instant to the whole second, with a `Z` | The page keeper refuses a file whose length differs from the size it was handed, `askCost()` prices a span before any fetch, and `ask()` refuses an oversized span before any fetch - all three need a size per file. `toISOString()` adds milliseconds, which `TIMESTAMP_PATTERN` in `backend/idhazh/contracts/base.py` refuses |
| 3 | **The build lists `.parquet` names only.** A day whose directory also holds a `.json` writer file - a name the contract allows, because the ledger door writes both formats - is left unlisted, and the build's log names the file | The engine reads parquet only. An unlisted day inside the listed range is a hole the door names (`file-missing`), which is better than an answer with rows silently missing |
| 4 | **Into the staged site only, never into `state/`** | "A listing in `state/` is the files one compaction read" stays true |
| 5 | **The door asks for a listing only for a day the build listed.** `vite.config.ts` computes `__RAW_LISTED_THROUGH__`, the newest listed day per ledger, through `frontend/scripts/raw-listed-through.mjs`, from the listings the copy step staged under `frontend/static/state/raw/`. A day after it is clamped away, as a day after `through` is today. A missing listing inside the listed range is `file-missing`, and the answer is `unreachable` at that day | A door that asked past the last listing and treated the 404 as normal is what row 3's rejected alternative 4 refuses, and the browser smoke counts every new 404 (CLAUDE.md section 12). The constant reads the staged tree rather than `STATE_ROOT`, so a canary build and a full build each read their own copy, and the walk keeps one writer |

**A writer file's address is composed from a checked name.** `slice-reader.ts` composes the raw paths, as it composes the compact ones, and only from a name that matches `FILE_ID_NAME_PATTERN` in `backend/idhazh/contracts/base.py`, carried into the frontend copy of `RawDayIndex` and bound by `test_frontend_index_shapes.py`; a listing naming anything else is refused by name, as an index with a bad `covers` is (Guardrail #11). **The frontend copy requires `bytes`, one size per name**, because the site build always writes it and a listing without it can be neither priced nor checked. The Python contract keeps it optional, so a listing the compaction wrote into `state/` still validates.

**A date in two tiers is a defect, not a merge.** Reading one date twice doubles every number on the answer. `filesFor()` already holds this property across the three packed periods and its docstring says so; the oracle in row 2 extends the assertion over a fixture ledger carrying packed and writer files for overlapping days.

**The writers' own files are not packed, so they cost more per day.** One request per writer file instead of one for the day: `host-fingerprint` wrote 55 files for 2026-09-30 alone (its committed listing). This is why panels stay on packed files: a panel draws on arrival for every reader, and this page fetches when an operator presses a button. `askCost()` reports the file count before the run, so the cost is visible at the moment it is chosen.

**Where the tiers disagree, the packed file wins by being the only one read.** Packing applies each ledger's own merge rule while it builds the file, so a packed day holds one row per record; the writers' files for an unpacked day hold what the writers wrote, which can be two rows of one record for a ledger whose merge rule is a cell union. **A difference that cannot be removed is printed on the page** rather than hidden: `AskResult.unpackedDays` carries the dates that came from the writers' tier, and the answer panel's note names them (section 2.11, the answer panel's note).

### 2.8 What reaches the site is capped at the widest span, not at what `state/` holds

**The publishing decision is site-wide; the cap on it is derived from config.** There is one staged tree, served at one address, and any page may fetch any of it - so this is not a per-route setting and no row here invents one. What the cap does is refuse to copy a file no page could ask for.

| # | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | The build copies, per published ledger, every compact file whose period overlaps the `max(console.window_presets)` UTC days ending on that ledger's newest packed day: the newest day any packed index names, with a day as itself, a month through its last UTC day and a year through 31 December. A period is copied whole, so the oldest day is rounded out to the coarsest file that holds it - a month file, or a whole year file once its year is packed. **The anchor is the data, never the build's clock.** The canary's packed days are fixed in August 2026 (`DATE` in `backend/utilities/build_canary_day.py`), and a cap counted back from the build's day would copy none of them from 2026-11-19 on |
| 2 | **The site's copy of each index names exactly the files the site carries.** The build stages each of the three indexes trimmed to the entries it copied, in the same `CompactIndex` shape and stamp, rather than byte for byte. A copied index that named a file the build left out would make the door report `file-missing` for a day nobody broke, and would fail `frontend/tests/published-ledgers.spec.ts`, which asks for every address an index names. The data files stay byte for byte, so a published file still cannot say anything `state/` does not. A trimmed index keeps the `CompactIndex` stamp, and `published-ledgers.spec.ts` reads every staged index through `readIndex()`, so the trimmed shape is checked against the contract the door reads |
| 3 | The writers' tier is staged only for days after the newest day any packed index names - a day as itself, a month through its last UTC day and a year through 31 December - and at most `max(console.window_presets)` of them, by row 7 (section 2.7, the listing rules) |
| 4 | **The number is read from config, never written down.** `published-ledgers.mjs` reads `console.window_presets` from `config/appearance.json`, where the key lives - the legacy `console` block in `config/idhazh.json`, the only file the script reads today, has no such key - as `build-frame-css.mjs` reads that file, and it refuses a missing key by name. It cannot call `consoleConfig()`: the copy step runs under plain Node before Vite, and `frontend/src/lib/server/config.ts` imports `./payload` with no extension, which plain Node does not resolve. Adding a wider preset widens the copy with no source edit, which is the substitution test (Guardrail #6) |
| 5 | **What `state/` holds is a different question and belongs to the gardener's keep windows.** Git keeps the history; the site carries the part a reader can reach. The gardener's loader already refuses a published ledger whose keep windows reach back less than the widest preset (`_refuse_a_published_reach_that_grows_or_falls_short` in `backend/idhazh/config.py`), so the cap never asks for a day `state/` has dropped |

**Past the cap, the page reads the repository, and no engine setting changes.** `state/` is committed, the repository is public, and a browser can read a committed file directly. So a date older than the oldest day the site's indexes name is fetched from the repository instead, chosen from the repository's own indexes, and only the address changes.

**Measured 2026-09-28**, against a committed file on `main` at the time (the file has since moved into the parquet tiers; the headers are the host's):

| # | What was read | Value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Repository visibility | public |
| 2 | Status for `<archive base>/state/host-fingerprint/2026/09/16/settled.csv` | 200, 350 bytes |
| 3 | `access-control-allow-origin` | `*` - so a browser may read it from our own page |
| 4 | `cache-control` | `max-age=300`. A packed file never changes, but this host re-validates after five minutes, so a repeated query over old dates pays the fetch again |
| 5 | `accept-ranges` | `bytes`. Noted and unused - the archive is read whole (rule 6 below) |

**The page fetches it; the engine never does.** `ledger.ts` makes a second page keeper, over a second `fetchedBytes()` rooted at the archive prefix and on the same engine, and hands that keeper the source **without its `address()`**, so it fetches every archive file whole - a year file included - and hands the engine one more buffer (`page-keeper.ts`: "a source with no address ... gets the file fetched whole instead"). No engine setting changes and no trigger fires.

| # | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | The address is `ledger.archive_base_url`, **a committed value in `config/idhazh.json`**, joined onto the same committed path. An environment value is not an acceptable control for a destination (Guardrail #11) |
| 2 | `connect-src` gains that one origin, computed from the same config value by a reader in `frontend/asset-base.js` and joined to the list `frontend/svelte.config.js` hands `connectSources()` - the pattern `visuals.asset_base_url` and `ledger.engine_extension_repository` already use. `asset-base.spec.ts` asserts that expression word for word, so the two files change together |
| 3 | The origin serves files and accepts none, so nothing can be sent to it; what it adds is that a query can read any public file on that host, which is already public |
| 4 | Where the value is empty the page reads the site only. **A span that starts before the oldest day the site's indexes name is read from that day on**: the earlier days are clamped away, as days after the newest listed day are, and the page says so in the neutral tone - `Days before {shortDate(day)} are not on this site.` It is not drawn as a failed fetch, because nothing failed (Susan, 2026-10-02). `SpanCost` and the `ok` and `quiet` answers gain `siteFrom: DateStamp \| null` for it: that oldest day when the span starts before it and no archive address is set, and `null` otherwise. **The empty default is what a fresh clone runs on** |
| 5 | The repository's own keep windows decide how far back this reaches. Where the repository no longer holds a date either, the answer is `unreachable` and names the date |
| 6 | The archive is read whole, never by byte range. Its host's answer to a `HEAD` that carries a `Range` was not measured, and the engine opens a ranged file only from that answer; a whole fetch is bounded by `maxFetchBytes` like any other |

**How a person reaches a day past the cap: custom UTC dates** (row 6 decision 9, owner, 2026-10-03). The shared span control offers preset shortcuts ending on the reader's UTC day, so without custom dates no question could ask for an older custom span. Row 6 adds two native date inputs to the question panel, labelled `From (UTC)` and `To (UTC)`, bounded to the reader's UTC day and the 364 UTC days before it by `console.explorer_reach_days`. A custom link carries `from=YYYY-MM-DD` and `end=YYYY-MM-DD` instead of `days`. Preset tiles stay as shortcuts: pressing one sets `To (UTC)` to today and `From (UTC)` to the preset's span ending today. Where the inputs stand is section 2.16 rule 5.

**What this buys, plainly: the site carries ninety days and the page can still answer a question about last spring.**

### 2.9 The ledger registry, as the page reads it

Row 3 copies `config/ledgers.json` to the site verbatim, at its committed path - `<site>/config/ledgers.json`, staged as `frontend/static/config/ledgers.json` - so the address the page asks for and the committed path are one string, as they are for a ledger file; row 4 fetches it. **It is an array of families, each holding an array of ledgers** - not a flat map, which is what a hand copy written from the name alone would assume.

```ts
/** The hand copy of the two levels the page reads. `LedgersConfig` in
 *  `backend/idhazh/contracts/ledgers.py` is the source of truth; a backend test binds this. */
export type LedgerFamily = {
	name: string;
	lifecycle_status: string;
	description: string;
	onboarded: string;
	ledgers: readonly { name: string; grain: string }[];
};
export type LedgerRegistry = { families: readonly LedgerFamily[] };
```

| # | Fact | Value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | How many families and ledgers | Not written here, because it moves: plan 58 is moving more ledgers as this is written. Count them at dispatch from `config/ledgers.json`. On 2026-10-02, after #1189 retired `span-rollup`, it declared 18 families holding 24 ledgers |
| 2 | Fields on a family and on a ledger | Family: `name`, `lifecycle_status`, `description`, `onboarded`, `ledgers`. Ledger: `name`, `grain`, `prefix`, `stem`, `suffix` - the page reads `name` and `grain` and ignores the rest |
| 3 | `ledger.published` in `config/idhazh.json` | On 2026-10-02: `host-fingerprint`, `item-health` and `summary-quality-evals`, published by #1169. Row 3 widens it |
| 4 | How the page knows the published list | `__PUBLISHED_LEDGERS__`, a build-time constant from `ledger.published` (section 2.3). It changes only with a deploy, and a deploy is what changes which files the site holds, so a constant cannot disagree with the files it describes. An open tab keeps the list it loaded with, as it keeps the indexes it read (`docs/architecture/publishing/how-the-query-door-answers-a-panel.md`, "What an open tab shows after a deploy") - on this page the indexes only until Refresh, below |
| 5 | How the page knows a ledger is not queryable | It is in the registry and not in the published list. **Never by probing for a 404** |

**How Refresh reaches the answer** (Fowler, 2026-10-02). The page keeper keeps every index for the page's life, and `ask()` reads through it, so a Refresh that re-read the indexes anywhere else would print a newer day than its answers use - and an index read outside the door fires ESCALATE trigger 6. So `ledger.ts` gains `startAfresh()`: it calls `release()` on this page's keeper and drops it, and the next `askCost()` and `ask()` make a new keeper that reads every index again. `page-keeper.ts`'s note that a page never releases its keeper changes to name this one caller. The registry itself is re-read by `registry.ts` with `cache: 'no-store'`.

**The registry is the one file of `config/` the site ships, and it is fetched rather than baked.** `docs/concepts/config.md`, "Build-time config versus shipped config", says a knob a surface needs is imported at build time, never fetched. The owner ruled the registry an exception on 2026-09-28 (row 3 decision 1): it is a list the page refreshes, not a knob. Row 3 writes the exception and its reason into that section.

**`grain` is the word the registry uses for how a ledger files** - `day`, `tree`, `month`, `flat`, `stamp`, `raw-and-compact`. **The page never reuses that word for a time bucket**; a time bucket is a `bin`. One word, one job (CLAUDE.md section 0b).

### 2.10 Every cell is text, and nothing on this page can change that

Guardrail #11. A ledger carries text that came off the open web - an article title, a source address, a server's error string.

| # | Rule | How it is held |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No `{@html}` anywhere under `frontend/src/routes/console/data-explorer/` or `frontend/src/lib/console/explorer/` | An import and source walk in the route's own spec |
| 2 | A cell is never a link, an image source, a fetch address or a style value, whatever it looks like | The spec renders a fixture row whose cells hold `<script>alert(1)</script>`, `javascript:alert(1)`, `=cmd\|' /c calc'!A1` and `https://example.invalid/x`, and asserts the rendered subtree has no `<a>`, no `<script>`, no `<img>`, and visible text equal to the cell |
| 3 | A column name comes from the engine's schema, never from a cell | `Column.name` is the only source the header reads |
| 4 | A ledger name in a fetch address or a view name comes from `LEDGER_NAMES`, never from typed text | The union is closed; `slice-reader.ts` composes the address and `ask-reader.ts` the view, each from a member of it |
| 5 | A copied cell stays text wherever it is pasted | `Copy as table` writes each cell as a Markdown code span, fenced by one more backtick than the cell's longest run of backticks, with each pipe character escaped by a backslash and each line break written as a space. `Copy as JSON` writes `JSON.stringify` of the rows. Without the code span, a cell holding `[x](https://...)` or `![x](...)` becomes a live link or image wherever the table is pasted (Fowler, 2026-10-02) |

**The spreadsheet-formula case is closed by refusing the download**, not by escaping: there is no file for a spreadsheet to open. The Markdown case is closed by rule 5.

### 2.11 The answer: the table, then the shape

**The answer panel**, `data-explorer-rows`, titled `The answer` (row 4). The words are Susan's and the places Jony's, both 2026-10-02. Top to bottom, the panel is: the heading line (the title, and from 1024 px the copy buttons at its right end, rule 10); the note (rule 5); below 1024 px, the copy buttons; the main figure (rule 7); the comparison line (rule 6); the table box (rules 1 to 4); then `Show more` and its count line (rule 8). The rows below are numbered for reference, not in that order.

| # | Part | Rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The table box | **The answer region is fixed at `console.explorer_answer_svh` percent of the small viewport height, and the table scrolls inside its own box, in both directions, with the header stuck to that box.** The panel is `wide` (rule 9), and the box, a direct child of the panel body, sets `padding-inline: 0`, so it runs from border to border and a sticky cell sticks where it stands at rest. It shows its scrollbars (`scrollbar-width: thin`). It carries `tabindex="0"`, `role="region"`, the label `Answer, {rows} rows by {cols} columns`, `data-chart="answer-table"` (sufficiency gate 1), and `data-readout-none="every value is printed in its own cell; agreed with Susan"` (gate 9). The page itself never scrolls sideways: at 390 px the document's `scrollWidth` is at most its `clientWidth`. The box and its sticky cells keep `--color-surface` (gate 2), and sticky cells carry a 1 px inner border on their right in `--color-rule-strong`. `.console-table` is not reused as it is: it sets `overflow-x: auto` and no height, so its sticky header sticks inside a box that never scrolls |
| 2 | Row numbers and sticky columns | The row-number column is `calc(4ch + var(--space-3) + var(--space-5))` wide - the last term so that at rest its numbers line up with the title - in `--font-data` and the tertiary colour, and sticks to the box's left edge at every width, so a reader who scrolls sideways still sees which row they are on. A row's number is its place in the answer as the engine returned it, so it moves with its row when a column is sorted. Row numbers are never copied. From 640 px (`frame.breakpoints_px[0]`) the first answer column also sticks, next to the row numbers; below 640 px only the row numbers stick. Susan proposed hiding the row numbers below 640 px and capping the box at 40rem; the layout is Jony's call |
| 3 | The header | Line 1 is the column's name exactly as a statement writes it, in `--font-data`, as a `<button>`. Line 2 is the engine's type name in lower case, in the tertiary colour: `{type}, full bar {max}` where the column draws bars (rule 6), and `timestamp, UTC` for a timestamp. `<th>` carries `aria-sort`. First press: numbers and dates sort high to low, text A to Z. Second press reverses. Third press returns to the engine's order. A `sort-descending` or `sort-ascending` icon follows the sorted name, `aria-hidden`. Nulls go last both ways. The sort runs over rows already in memory - no second fetch and no second statement. The focus ring sits inside the button (`outline-offset: -2px`), because the scroll box would clip a ring outside it, as the tab strip's does |
| 4 | How a cell prints | **By `Column.type`, never by the JavaScript value** (section 2.5 rule 8). NULL: `null`, in the tertiary colour, italic. Integers: `grouped()` from five digits up, so 2026 stays 2026. DOUBLE and DECIMAL: up to 3 decimals, trailing zeros dropped; a non-zero value under 0.001 in e-notation with 3 significant figures. DATE: `2026-10-02`. TIMESTAMP: `2026-10-02 08:24:32`, with milliseconds only when they are not zero. BOOLEAN: `true` or `false`. LIST, STRUCT and MAP: their JSON text, in `--font-data`. BLOB: `{n} bytes`. An integer past 2^53: its exact digits, and no bar. An empty string prints nothing. Numbers are right-aligned in tabular figures. A text cell wraps at `console.explorer_cell_max_ch` characters with `overflow-wrap: anywhere` and is never cut short |
| 5 | The note, the one place for facts about the answer | At most three sentences, in this order, each only when true. The answer's own span: `Read from {d} UTC days, {dayMonth(from)} to {shortDate(to)}.` - the span this run read, so moving the span control later does not change it. Capped (`AskResult.capped`): `The answer stopped at {max} rows, the most one answer holds here. Narrow the days or the question to see the rest.` Days not packed yet (`unpackedDays`): `{days} come from the files the pipeline wrote that day, not yet merged into one file a day, so a record written twice there can appear twice.` What the run fetched, and how long it took, stand in the action line (section 2.16 rule 5), not here |
| 6 | The in-cell bar | A column draws bars only when every value is zero or more, the largest is above zero, and the smallest is at most `console.explorer_bar_spread_share` (0.5) of the largest - so a year column draws none. A bar is 3 px tall, under the digits, from the cell's left edge, `--radius-full`, in `--chart-1` (5.9:1 on the dark surface, 6.4:1 on the light). The ranked list's bar uses the same colour, so a size bar means one thing everywhere. Zero draws an empty bar; the column's largest value draws a full one. Where any column draws bars, a line above the table reads `Each bar compares a number against the largest in its column.`, carrying `data-comparison="each number against the largest in its column"`. Where no column draws bars, the same line reads `Press a column's name to sort the rows by it.`, carrying `data-comparison="each row against the others, in the order of the column you sort by"`, so the panel always carries exactly one comparison |
| 7 | The main figure | `{rows} rows` (`1 row` for one, and `The first {rows} rows` when the answer is capped), in `--text-xl` (22 px, against the 18 px title), carrying `data-lede`. The table's label and rule 8's sentences also say `row` for one |
| 8 | Show more | Under the table box: `Show {n} more rows`, which adds `console.explorer_row_page` rows, then `{shown} of {rows} rows shown.` When every row shows there is no button, and the line reads `All {rows} rows are shown.` The answer itself stops at `console.explorer_max_rows` (the capped sentence in rule 5) |
| 9 | Width | **The answer panel and the chart panel are wide panels** (`Panel`'s own `wide` prop, which runs the body from border to border and pads each direct child of it by `--space-5`, 24 px), so the table's box reaches both borders and the chart keeps the inset every console chart has. **There is no widen control**: every console panel already spans the frame. What the reader loses: on a screen wider than 1,600 px, where the frame stops, a wide answer scrolls sideways inside its box instead of spreading out |
| 10 | Copy | `Copy as JSON` and `Copy as table`, each with the `copy` icon, at the right end of the panel's heading line, in `Panel`'s `actions` snippet (row 5); below 1024 px (`frame.breakpoints_px[1]`) they stand under the note.
